    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n9\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF41.9, F31.9, F20.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, it’s good to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Hi, yes, nice to meet you too. Thank you so much for fitting me in. \n\nWell, as to what brings me in... it's a bit embarrassing to admit at my age, honestly. I'm sixty, and I've been a high school teacher for decades—mostly history, some government classes—so you would really think I'd have mastered the art of sitting through a long meeting by now. But I just... I keep getting up. That's the main thing. I keep getting up in situations where everyone else is perfectly content being seated, and it's starting to become a real issue for me. \n\nLike, if I'm in a faculty meeting, everyone else is just sitting there, listening to the principal or taking notes, but I will suddenly feel like I absolutely have to stand. I'll make up little excuses to myself, you know? I'll think, \"Oh, I need to refill my coffee right now,\" even if my cup is still half full, or I'll get up to check a stack of handouts on the back table, or I'll just walk to the back of the room and stand by the door, even though nothing is really urgent at all. \n\nAnd it’s not just at school, it’s in my personal life too. At dinner! My husband and I will be eating, maybe we even have friends over for a nice meal, and everyone is sitting and chatting, but I'll just pop up out of my chair. I'll go check the oven, or look for a different serving spoon, or just stand by the kitchen island for a few minutes. My husband actually joked the other night that I’m like a jack-in-the-box, which, well, it made me laugh at first, but then it made me realize how much I'm actually doing it. It makes other people feel rushed or distracted, I think. I just can't seem to stay planted in my seat when I'm supposed to, and I really don't understand why I can't just sit through a meal or a meeting like a normal adult. So, that's why I'm here.\n\nDoctor: When you’re sitting and feel you have to get up, do you also get an uncomfortable feeling in your legs that eases when you stand or walk?\n\nPatient: No, no, it's not like that. I don't have any sort of physical ache or tingling or anything creepy-crawly like that. My legs themselves feel completely fine. There's no pain, no discomfort in the muscles or the joints or anything of that nature. It's more just... well, it's this overwhelming sense that I shouldn't be sitting anymore. Like the chair is suddenly just the wrong place to be. \n\nI'll just be sitting there, and my legs are perfectly comfortable, but my brain is just saying, \"Okay, time to get up now, let's go.\" It's almost like a spring uncoiling, but not in a physical or painful way, if that makes sense? Just a sudden, absolute necessity to be vertical. Like yesterday, I was grading papers at the kitchen table—just AP History essays, which take forever, honestly, because the kids write these sprawling paragraphs and you really have to focus to follow their arguments—and my legs were totally fine, just resting on the rung of the stool. But I just had to stand up. I ended up taking the whole stack of rubrics and papers over to the island to grade standing up for the next twenty minutes. \n\nI didn't feel any physical relief in my legs when I stood, because they didn't hurt or feel restless to begin with, but I just felt... better, overall, being up. Like I was finally doing what I was supposed to be doing. So, no, definitely no uncomfortable feeling in my legs specifically. It's not a physical ache at all.\n\nDoctor: When you're expected to stay seated, do you find your mind wandering so much that getting up helps you refocus?\n\nPatient: Well, no, I wouldn't say it's about refocusing, exactly. It's not like I'm sitting there and my mind is drifting off to, oh, I don't know, what I'm going to make for dinner or my grocery list or wondering if I left the porch light on. I mean, sure, everyone's mind wanders a bit, especially during those long professional development days where they bring in some consultant who hasn't been in a classroom since 1995 to tell us how to engage teenagers. But it's not that I'm losing my train of thought and standing up brings it back. \n\nIt's actually almost the opposite? Like, I can be completely focused on what's happening. I could be totally engrossed in a student's presentation on the Industrial Revolution, or right in the middle of a really good conversation with my husband about our weekend plans, and I still just have to get up. My brain is right there in the moment, fully engaged, but my body just... rebels against the chair. \n\nI remember last week, we had a faculty meeting about the new grading software, which, let me tell you, is a nightmare to navigate, but I was actually really trying to pay attention because I needed to know how to input my midterm grades. I was taking notes, I was following along on my laptop, I was completely dialed in. And yet, I still found myself standing up and migrating to the back of the library. I didn't stand up to concentrate better on the software demonstration; I just stood up because I couldn't sit anymore. And honestly, sometimes getting up actually distracts me more because then I have to think of a reason why I'm standing, like pretending to look at the bulletin board, instead of just focusing on the meeting. So, no, I don't think it's a matter of my mind wandering and needing to reset my focus. It's just... the sitting itself that I can't seem to tolerate for very long.\n\nDoctor: When you have to get up, do you feel an inner restlessness?\n\nPatient: Well, an inner restlessness... I suppose that's one way to put it, though I'm not entirely sure if \"restlessness\" captures the whole picture, or maybe it captures it too perfectly? It's hard to explain without sounding a bit dramatic. But yes, I suppose there is this... this buzzing feeling inside. It’s not physical, like I said about my legs, but it's like my engine is just idling too high. \n\nI remember trying to explain this to my sister once—she’s a retired librarian, very calm, very sedate, could sit in a wingback chair with a cup of tea for six hours straight and not twitch once. I told her it feels like I have a motor running inside me that I can’t quite shut off. Even when I’m sitting, even when I’m perfectly comfortable, there’s this low-level hum of energy that just builds and builds. It’s like when you’re waiting for water to boil, and you see those little bubbles starting at the bottom of the pot, and you know it’s about to roil over. That’s what it feels like inside my chest or my head, just this constant, low-grade need for motion. \n\nIt’s exhausting sometimes, to be honest. Like last weekend, I was sitting on the patio, beautiful weather, a lovely breeze, reading a biography of Eleanor Roosevelt—fascinating woman, really, the amount of energy she had was astounding—and I should have been perfectly relaxed. But inside, I just felt this... this tightness. This urge to do something, anything, other than just sit there. I ended up getting up to deadhead the petunias, then I swept the porch, then I went inside to reorganize the Tupperware drawer, all because I couldn't just sit with that feeling. So, yes, I suppose \"inner restlessness\" is a good way to describe it, though it feels more like an internal pressure that just demands I get moving.\n\nDoctor: During these times, do you feel unusually euphoric or excessively upbeat?\n\nPatient: No, no, definitely not. It's not a happy feeling at all. I wouldn't call it euphoric or upbeat in the slightest. It's actually quite the opposite, really. It's more of an annoyance, a frustration that I can't just be still like everyone else seems to be able to do. \n\nI mean, when I'm up and moving because of that internal pressure, I'm not doing it with a spring in my step or a song in my heart. I'm not whistling while I work, like some cheerful cartoon character. Take that time I was reorganizing the Tupperware drawer—I wasn't thrilled to be doing it. I was actually quite irritated because I *wanted* to be sitting on the patio reading about Eleanor Roosevelt. I *wanted* to be relaxing. But I was practically throwing the plastic lids around because I was just so aggravated by this need to be doing something. \n\nMy husband, bless him, sometimes he’ll see me pacing around the living room during a movie—we were trying to watch that new historical drama on PBS last night, beautifully costumed, really quite stunning, but I couldn't stay on the sofa for more than twenty minutes—and he'll ask if I'm excited about something. And I have to tell him, no, I'm not excited, I'm just... compelled. It's not a joyful energy. It's just a relentless one. So, no, there's no euphoria involved at all. Just a lot of sighing and wishing I could just sit still and enjoy a quiet moment without my body deciding it's time to run a marathon.\n\nDoctor: When this urge to get up happens, do you feel anxious?\n\nPatient: Anxious? Well... I suppose it depends on how you define anxious, doesn't it? I mean, I'm not sitting there worrying about, oh, whether I left the stove on, or if my students are going to fail their state exams—though, frankly, with the way the curriculum is structured these days, that's a valid concern for any teacher. But it's not a fearful anxiety. It's not like my heart is pounding because I'm scared of something or because I'm having a panic attack. I've had colleagues who get panic attacks, and it sounds just awful, but this isn't that.\n\nBut, on the other hand, if you mean anxious as in... keyed up, or agitated? Then maybe yes. Because when I can't get up, when I'm forced to just sit there and endure that buzzing feeling I told you about, I do start to feel a bit frantic. Like, I remember being at my niece's graduation ceremony last spring. Oh, it was lovely, she looked beautiful, valedictorian and everything, but the ceremony was *three hours long*. Three hours! Sitting on those hard metal folding chairs in a stuffy gymnasium. And by the second hour, when they were only halfway through the M's, I was practically crawling out of my skin. I wasn't worried or scared, but I was so incredibly tense and agitated that I felt like I was going to scream if I couldn't stand up and stretch my legs. I kept crossing and uncrossing my ankles, shifting my weight, checking my watch every two minutes. \n\nMy sister kept giving me these side-eyes, you know the look, the \"stop fidgeting, you're embarrassing me\" look. And that made me feel a little stressed, I suppose, because I knew I was being disruptive, but I just couldn't help it. The longer I had to suppress the urge to move, the more unbearable it became. So, it's not a psychological anxiety, where my mind is racing with worries. It's more of a physical distress, a desperate need for release. I don't know if that's what you mean by anxious, but that's the closest I can get to describing it. It's just this overwhelming, physical imperative to move, and when I can't, it's sheer torture.\n\nDoctor: Did this problem start after you began a new medication or changed the dose of one?\n\nPatient: No, no, nothing like that. I haven't started any new medications recently, nor have I changed the dose of anything I'm currently taking. I mean, I take a daily multivitamin, and calcium for my bones—you know, at my age, you really have to start thinking about osteoporosis, especially since my mother had it quite terribly in her later years, shrinking down to practically nothing, poor thing. And I take a little something for my blood pressure, just a very low dose that Dr. Evans prescribed about five years ago, but that's been perfectly stable. \n\nI haven't tweaked a thing. I'm very particular about following instructions when it comes to medications. I have one of those little pill organizers, the ones with the days of the week printed on them in big letters? Sunday through Saturday. I keep it right next to the coffee maker so I don't forget. So, no, it's definitely not a medication issue. This feeling, this... constant motor running, it's not a new development tied to a pill. It's just... well, it's just how I've been. It's not a side effect of anything I'm swallowing in the morning with my orange juice, I can assure you of that.\n\nDoctor: Have you been needing much less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: Less sleep? Oh, no, not at all. I mean, I certainly wouldn't say I *need* less sleep. If anything, I wish I could get more of it! I still aim for my solid eight hours every single night. I'm quite disciplined about it, really. I try to be in bed by 10:00 PM, lights out by 10:30 PM after I've read a chapter or two of whatever biography I'm currently absorbed in—right now it's a fascinating one on Abigail Adams, she really was quite ahead of her time, you know. \n\nBut my sleep hasn't changed. I still feel tired at the end of the day, just like anyone else. Teaching teenagers all day takes it out of you, let me tell you! By the time I've graded the last essay and prepped the lesson plan for the next morning, I am more than ready to put my head on the pillow. It's not like I'm staying up until three in the morning scrubbing the baseboards or feeling like I could run a marathon. I don't have that kind of... limitless energy where I just don't feel the need to rest. \n\nNow, actually *falling* asleep, well, sometimes that takes a minute because my mind might still be going over the events of the day, or I might be tossing and turning a bit because my legs feel restless—like they haven't gotten the memo that it's time to power down. But the *need* for sleep? Oh, yes, that is very much still there. If I don't get my eight hours, I am absolutely dragging the next day in the classroom, and you simply cannot be sluggish when you're trying to explain the intricacies of the American Revolution to thirty 11th graders. They will eat you alive! So, no, my sleep requirements haven't decreased in the slightest. I need every minute of rest I can get.\n\nDoctor: Has anyone told you that your behavior or speech becomes disorganized or hard to follow?\n\nPatient: Disorganized? Hard to follow? Well... I don't think so. I mean, certainly not in a way that anyone has ever felt the need to point out to me as a problem. As a teacher, you have to be organized in your speech, don't you? You have to be able to lay out a lesson plan clearly—first we discuss the causes, then the major battles, then the aftermath. If you're disorganized, the students just check out immediately. They'll start looking at their phones under the desks, or staring out the window, and you've lost them entirely. \n\nNow, do I sometimes go off on a tangent? Oh, absolutely. I'll be talking about the Boston Tea Party, and then suddenly I'm reminded of this fascinating article I read about the history of tea smuggling in the 18th century, and how it impacted the East India Company, and before I know it, I've spent five minutes talking about maritime trade routes instead of the Sons of Liberty. But I always bring it back around! It's just... I get enthusiastic, you know? There's so much interesting context, and I want them to see the whole picture, not just memorize a list of dates. \n\nMy husband, Arthur—bless him—he sometimes jokes that I take the scenic route when I'm telling a story. Like the other day, I was trying to tell him about a disagreement two of my colleagues were having in the staff room over the new grading policy, and I started explaining the background of the policy, and then how the principal introduced it at the last faculty meeting, and what she was wearing—she had this awful mustard yellow scarf on, I don't know who told her that was her color—and Arthur just sort of chuckled and said, \"Darling, I think we've lost the plot.\" But he was just teasing. It wasn't that I wasn't making sense, I was just giving him all the details! \n\nSo, no, no one has ever told me my speech is actually disorganized or impossible to follow. I might talk a lot, and I might weave a few different threads together, but it all makes sense in the end. I'm not speaking gibberish or jumping from one completely unrelated thought to another without any connection. It's just a very... detailed way of communicating, I suppose.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
An outside force? Oh, heavens, no. Nothing like that. It's not as though I'm being puppeted on strings or... or possessed by some sort of spirit, if that's what you're asking! It's entirely me. It's completely internal. 

It's just this... this build-up of energy, I suppose you could say. It comes from right inside my own bones. Like just last week, we had this dreadful professional development seminar—two solid hours on the new standardized testing protocols, and they had us sitting in those awful, rigid plastic folding chairs in the gymnasium. You know the ones, they absolutely murder your lower back. And about forty-five minutes in, my legs just started to feel... fizzy. That's really the best word for it. Fizzy, like there was carbonation right under my skin. 

And my own brain was just telling me, "You need to stand up, you need to stretch your calves right this very second or you are going to simply burst." It's my own body demanding it. There's no mysterious entity pulling the levers or forcing me out of the chair, it's just my own absolute inability to stay glued to a seat for hours on end without getting terribly uncomfortable. 

I mean, even at home, Arthur and I were watching a documentary on the Apollo missions the other night—fascinating stuff, really, the engineering involved is just staggering when you think about the technology they had in the sixties—and even in my own perfectly comfortable recliner in my own living room, I eventually had to get up and pace behind the sofa for a bit while we watched. Arthur didn't mind, he's used to it by now, but it was just me wanting to move. Not some invisible hand pushing me up. So, no, definitely no outside forces at work here. Just my own restless self.

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n10\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF41.9, F31.9, F20.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, it’s good to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Hi, yes, nice to meet you too. Thank you so much for fitting me in. \n\nWell, as to what brings me in... it's a bit embarrassing to admit at my age, honestly. I'm sixty, and I've been a high school teacher for decades—mostly history, some government classes—so you would really think I'd have mastered the art of sitting through a long meeting by now. But I just... I keep getting up. That's the main thing. I keep getting up in situations where everyone else is perfectly content being seated, and it's starting to become a real issue for me. \n\nLike, if I'm in a faculty meeting, everyone else is just sitting there, listening to the principal or taking notes, but I will suddenly feel like I absolutely have to stand. I'll make up little excuses to myself, you know? I'll think, \"Oh, I need to refill my coffee right now,\" even if my cup is still half full, or I'll get up to check a stack of handouts on the back table, or I'll just walk to the back of the room and stand by the door, even though nothing is really urgent at all. \n\nAnd it’s not just at school, it’s in my personal life too. At dinner! My husband and I will be eating, maybe we even have friends over for a nice meal, and everyone is sitting and chatting, but I'll just pop up out of my chair. I'll go check the oven, or look for a different serving spoon, or just stand by the kitchen island for a few minutes. My husband actually joked the other night that I’m like a jack-in-the-box, which, well, it made me laugh at first, but then it made me realize how much I'm actually doing it. It makes other people feel rushed or distracted, I think. I just can't seem to stay planted in my seat when I'm supposed to, and I really don't understand why I can't just sit through a meal or a meeting like a normal adult. So, that's why I'm here.\n\nDoctor: When you’re sitting and feel you have to get up, do you also get an uncomfortable feeling in your legs that eases when you stand or walk?\n\nPatient: No, no, it's not like that. I don't have any sort of physical ache or tingling or anything creepy-crawly like that. My legs themselves feel completely fine. There's no pain, no discomfort in the muscles or the joints or anything of that nature. It's more just... well, it's this overwhelming sense that I shouldn't be sitting anymore. Like the chair is suddenly just the wrong place to be. \n\nI'll just be sitting there, and my legs are perfectly comfortable, but my brain is just saying, \"Okay, time to get up now, let's go.\" It's almost like a spring uncoiling, but not in a physical or painful way, if that makes sense? Just a sudden, absolute necessity to be vertical. Like yesterday, I was grading papers at the kitchen table—just AP History essays, which take forever, honestly, because the kids write these sprawling paragraphs and you really have to focus to follow their arguments—and my legs were totally fine, just resting on the rung of the stool. But I just had to stand up. I ended up taking the whole stack of rubrics and papers over to the island to grade standing up for the next twenty minutes. \n\nI didn't feel any physical relief in my legs when I stood, because they didn't hurt or feel restless to begin with, but I just felt... better, overall, being up. Like I was finally doing what I was supposed to be doing. So, no, definitely no uncomfortable feeling in my legs specifically. It's not a physical ache at all.\n\nDoctor: When you're expected to stay seated, do you find your mind wandering so much that getting up helps you refocus?\n\nPatient: Well, no, I wouldn't say it's about refocusing, exactly. It's not like I'm sitting there and my mind is drifting off to, oh, I don't know, what I'm going to make for dinner or my grocery list or wondering if I left the porch light on. I mean, sure, everyone's mind wanders a bit, especially during those long professional development days where they bring in some consultant who hasn't been in a classroom since 1995 to tell us how to engage teenagers. But it's not that I'm losing my train of thought and standing up brings it back. \n\nIt's actually almost the opposite? Like, I can be completely focused on what's happening. I could be totally engrossed in a student's presentation on the Industrial Revolution, or right in the middle of a really good conversation with my husband about our weekend plans, and I still just have to get up. My brain is right there in the moment, fully engaged, but my body just... rebels against the chair. \n\nI remember last week, we had a faculty meeting about the new grading software, which, let me tell you, is a nightmare to navigate, but I was actually really trying to pay attention because I needed to know how to input my midterm grades. I was taking notes, I was following along on my laptop, I was completely dialed in. And yet, I still found myself standing up and migrating to the back of the library. I didn't stand up to concentrate better on the software demonstration; I just stood up because I couldn't sit anymore. And honestly, sometimes getting up actually distracts me more because then I have to think of a reason why I'm standing, like pretending to look at the bulletin board, instead of just focusing on the meeting. So, no, I don't think it's a matter of my mind wandering and needing to reset my focus. It's just... the sitting itself that I can't seem to tolerate for very long.\n\nDoctor: When you have to get up, do you feel an inner restlessness?\n\nPatient: Well, an inner restlessness... I suppose that's one way to put it, though I'm not entirely sure if \"restlessness\" captures the whole picture, or maybe it captures it too perfectly? It's hard to explain without sounding a bit dramatic. But yes, I suppose there is this... this buzzing feeling inside. It’s not physical, like I said about my legs, but it's like my engine is just idling too high. \n\nI remember trying to explain this to my sister once—she’s a retired librarian, very calm, very sedate, could sit in a wingback chair with a cup of tea for six hours straight and not twitch once. I told her it feels like I have a motor running inside me that I can’t quite shut off. Even when I’m sitting, even when I’m perfectly comfortable, there’s this low-level hum of energy that just builds and builds. It’s like when you’re waiting for water to boil, and you see those little bubbles starting at the bottom of the pot, and you know it’s about to roil over. That’s what it feels like inside my chest or my head, just this constant, low-grade need for motion. \n\nIt’s exhausting sometimes, to be honest. Like last weekend, I was sitting on the patio, beautiful weather, a lovely breeze, reading a biography of Eleanor Roosevelt—fascinating woman, really, the amount of energy she had was astounding—and I should have been perfectly relaxed. But inside, I just felt this... this tightness. This urge to do something, anything, other than just sit there. I ended up getting up to deadhead the petunias, then I swept the porch, then I went inside to reorganize the Tupperware drawer, all because I couldn't just sit with that feeling. So, yes, I suppose \"inner restlessness\" is a good way to describe it, though it feels more like an internal pressure that just demands I get moving.\n\nDoctor: During these times, do you feel unusually euphoric or excessively upbeat?\n\nPatient: No, no, definitely not. It's not a happy feeling at all. I wouldn't call it euphoric or upbeat in the slightest. It's actually quite the opposite, really. It's more of an annoyance, a frustration that I can't just be still like everyone else seems to be able to do. \n\nI mean, when I'm up and moving because of that internal pressure, I'm not doing it with a spring in my step or a song in my heart. I'm not whistling while I work, like some cheerful cartoon character. Take that time I was reorganizing the Tupperware drawer—I wasn't thrilled to be doing it. I was actually quite irritated because I *wanted* to be sitting on the patio reading about Eleanor Roosevelt. I *wanted* to be relaxing. But I was practically throwing the plastic lids around because I was just so aggravated by this need to be doing something. \n\nMy husband, bless him, sometimes he’ll see me pacing around the living room during a movie—we were trying to watch that new historical drama on PBS last night, beautifully costumed, really quite stunning, but I couldn't stay on the sofa for more than twenty minutes—and he'll ask if I'm excited about something. And I have to tell him, no, I'm not excited, I'm just... compelled. It's not a joyful energy. It's just a relentless one. So, no, there's no euphoria involved at all. Just a lot of sighing and wishing I could just sit still and enjoy a quiet moment without my body deciding it's time to run a marathon.\n\nDoctor: When this urge to get up happens, do you feel anxious?\n\nPatient: Anxious? Well... I suppose it depends on how you define anxious, doesn't it? I mean, I'm not sitting there worrying about, oh, whether I left the stove on, or if my students are going to fail their state exams—though, frankly, with the way the curriculum is structured these days, that's a valid concern for any teacher. But it's not a fearful anxiety. It's not like my heart is pounding because I'm scared of something or because I'm having a panic attack. I've had colleagues who get panic attacks, and it sounds just awful, but this isn't that.\n\nBut, on the other hand, if you mean anxious as in... keyed up, or agitated? Then maybe yes. Because when I can't get up, when I'm forced to just sit there and endure that buzzing feeling I told you about, I do start to feel a bit frantic. Like, I remember being at my niece's graduation ceremony last spring. Oh, it was lovely, she looked beautiful, valedictorian and everything, but the ceremony was *three hours long*. Three hours! Sitting on those hard metal folding chairs in a stuffy gymnasium. And by the second hour, when they were only halfway through the M's, I was practically crawling out of my skin. I wasn't worried or scared, but I was so incredibly tense and agitated that I felt like I was going to scream if I couldn't stand up and stretch my legs. I kept crossing and uncrossing my ankles, shifting my weight, checking my watch every two minutes. \n\nMy sister kept giving me these side-eyes, you know the look, the \"stop fidgeting, you're embarrassing me\" look. And that made me feel a little stressed, I suppose, because I knew I was being disruptive, but I just couldn't help it. The longer I had to suppress the urge to move, the more unbearable it became. So, it's not a psychological anxiety, where my mind is racing with worries. It's more of a physical distress, a desperate need for release. I don't know if that's what you mean by anxious, but that's the closest I can get to describing it. It's just this overwhelming, physical imperative to move, and when I can't, it's sheer torture.\n\nDoctor: Did this problem start after you began a new medication or changed the dose of one?\n\nPatient: No, no, nothing like that. I haven't started any new medications recently, nor have I changed the dose of anything I'm currently taking. I mean, I take a daily multivitamin, and calcium for my bones—you know, at my age, you really have to start thinking about osteoporosis, especially since my mother had it quite terribly in her later years, shrinking down to practically nothing, poor thing. And I take a little something for my blood pressure, just a very low dose that Dr. Evans prescribed about five years ago, but that's been perfectly stable. \n\nI haven't tweaked a thing. I'm very particular about following instructions when it comes to medications. I have one of those little pill organizers, the ones with the days of the week printed on them in big letters? Sunday through Saturday. I keep it right next to the coffee maker so I don't forget. So, no, it's definitely not a medication issue. This feeling, this... constant motor running, it's not a new development tied to a pill. It's just... well, it's just how I've been. It's not a side effect of anything I'm swallowing in the morning with my orange juice, I can assure you of that.\n\nDoctor: Have you been needing much less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: Less sleep? Oh, no, not at all. I mean, I certainly wouldn't say I *need* less sleep. If anything, I wish I could get more of it! I still aim for my solid eight hours every single night. I'm quite disciplined about it, really. I try to be in bed by 10:00 PM, lights out by 10:30 PM after I've read a chapter or two of whatever biography I'm currently absorbed in—right now it's a fascinating one on Abigail Adams, she really was quite ahead of her time, you know. \n\nBut my sleep hasn't changed. I still feel tired at the end of the day, just like anyone else. Teaching teenagers all day takes it out of you, let me tell you! By the time I've graded the last essay and prepped the lesson plan for the next morning, I am more than ready to put my head on the pillow. It's not like I'm staying up until three in the morning scrubbing the baseboards or feeling like I could run a marathon. I don't have that kind of... limitless energy where I just don't feel the need to rest. \n\nNow, actually *falling* asleep, well, sometimes that takes a minute because my mind might still be going over the events of the day, or I might be tossing and turning a bit because my legs feel restless—like they haven't gotten the memo that it's time to power down. But the *need* for sleep? Oh, yes, that is very much still there. If I don't get my eight hours, I am absolutely dragging the next day in the classroom, and you simply cannot be sluggish when you're trying to explain the intricacies of the American Revolution to thirty 11th graders. They will eat you alive! So, no, my sleep requirements haven't decreased in the slightest. I need every minute of rest I can get.\n\nDoctor: Has anyone told you that your behavior or speech becomes disorganized or hard to follow?\n\nPatient: Disorganized? Hard to follow? Well... I don't think so. I mean, certainly not in a way that anyone has ever felt the need to point out to me as a problem. As a teacher, you have to be organized in your speech, don't you? You have to be able to lay out a lesson plan clearly—first we discuss the causes, then the major battles, then the aftermath. If you're disorganized, the students just check out immediately. They'll start looking at their phones under the desks, or staring out the window, and you've lost them entirely. \n\nNow, do I sometimes go off on a tangent? Oh, absolutely. I'll be talking about the Boston Tea Party, and then suddenly I'm reminded of this fascinating article I read about the history of tea smuggling in the 18th century, and how it impacted the East India Company, and before I know it, I've spent five minutes talking about maritime trade routes instead of the Sons of Liberty. But I always bring it back around! It's just... I get enthusiastic, you know? There's so much interesting context, and I want them to see the whole picture, not just memorize a list of dates. \n\nMy husband, Arthur—bless him—he sometimes jokes that I take the scenic route when I'm telling a story. Like the other day, I was trying to tell him about a disagreement two of my colleagues were having in the staff room over the new grading policy, and I started explaining the background of the policy, and then how the principal introduced it at the last faculty meeting, and what she was wearing—she had this awful mustard yellow scarf on, I don't know who told her that was her color—and Arthur just sort of chuckled and said, \"Darling, I think we've lost the plot.\" But he was just teasing. It wasn't that I wasn't making sense, I was just giving him all the details! \n\nSo, no, no one has ever told me my speech is actually disorganized or impossible to follow. I might talk a lot, and I might weave a few different threads together, but it all makes sense in the end. I'm not speaking gibberish or jumping from one completely unrelated thought to another without any connection. It's just a very... detailed way of communicating, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: When you feel compelled to stand up, does it ever seem like some outside force is making you move?\n\nPatient: An outside force? Oh, heavens, no. Nothing like that. It's not as though I'm being puppeted on strings or... or possessed by some sort of spirit, if that's what you're asking! It's entirely me. It's completely internal. \n\nIt's just this... this build-up of energy, I suppose you could say. It comes from right inside my own bones. Like just last week, we had this dreadful professional development seminar—two solid hours on the new standardized testing protocols, and they had us sitting in those awful, rigid plastic folding chairs in the gymnasium. You know the ones, they absolutely murder your lower back. And about forty-five minutes in, my legs just started to feel... fizzy. That's really the best word for it. Fizzy, like there was carbonation right under my skin. \n\nAnd my own brain was just telling me, \"You need to stand up, you need to stretch your calves right this very second or you are going to simply burst.\" It's my own body demanding it. There's no mysterious entity pulling the levers or forcing me out of the chair, it's just my own absolute inability to stay glued to a seat for hours on end without getting terribly uncomfortable. \n\nI mean, even at home, Arthur and I were watching a documentary on the Apollo missions the other night—fascinating stuff, really, the engineering involved is just staggering when you think about the technology they had in the sixties—and even in my own perfectly comfortable recliner in my own living room, I eventually had to get up and pace behind the sofa for a bit while we watched. Arthur didn't mind, he's used to it by now, but it was just me wanting to move. Not some invisible hand pushing me up. So, no, definitely no outside forces at work here. Just my own restless self.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. You know the ones, they absolutely murder your lower back. And about forty-five minutes in, my legs just started to feel... fizzy. That's really the best word for it. Fizzy, like there was carbonation right under my skin. \n\nAnd my own brain was just telling me, \"You need to stand up, you need to stretch your calves right this very second or you are going to simply burst.\" It's my own body demanding it. There's no mysterious entity pulling the levers or forcing me out of the chair, it's just my own absolute inability to stay glued to a seat for hours on end without getting terribly uncomfortable. \n\nI mean, even at home, Arthur and I were watching a documentary on the Apollo missions the other night—fascinating stuff, really, the engineering involved is just staggering when you think about the technology they had in the sixties—and even in my own perfectly comfortable recliner in my own living room, I eventually had to get up and pace behind the sofa for a bit while we watched. Arthur didn't mind, he's used to it by now, but it was just me wanting to move. Not some invisible hand pushing me up. So, no, definitely no outside forces at work here. Just my own restless self."
  }
]

Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank. Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety. Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings). I'm sixty, and I've been a high school teacher for decades—mostly history, some government classes—so you would really think I'd have mastered the art of sitting through a long meeting by now. But I just... I keep getting up. That's the main thing. I keep getting up in situations where everyone else is perfectly content being seated, and it's starting to become a real issue for me. \n\nLike, if I'm in a faculty meeting, everyone else is just sitting there, listening to the principal or taking notes, but I will suddenly feel like I absolutely have to stand. I'll make up little excuses to myself, you know? I'll think, \"Oh, I need to refill my coffee right now,\" even if my cup is still half full, or I'll get up to check a stack of handouts on the back table, or I'll just walk to the back of the room and stand by the door, even though nothing is really urgent at all. \n\nAnd it’s not just at school, it’s in my personal life too. At dinner! My husband and I will be eating, maybe we even have friends over for a nice meal, and everyone is sitting and chatting, but I'll just pop up out of my chair. I'll go check the oven, or look for a different serving spoon, or just stand by the kitchen island for a few minutes. My husband actually joked the other night that I’m like a jack-in-the-box, which, well, it made me laugh at first, but then it made me realize how much I'm actually doing it. It makes other people feel rushed or distracted, I think. I just can't seem to stay planted in my seat when I'm supposed to, and I really don't understand why I can't just sit through a meal or a meeting like a normal adult. Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nNo, no, it's not like that. I don't have any sort of physical ache or tingling or anything creepy-crawly like that. My legs themselves feel completely fine. There's no pain, no discomfort in the muscles or the joints or anything of that nature. It's more just... well, it's this overwhelming sense that I shouldn't be sitting anymore. Like the chair is suddenly just the wrong place to be. \n\nI'll just be sitting there, and my legs are perfectly comfortable, but my brain is just saying, \"Okay, time to get up now, let's go.\" It's almost like a spring uncoiling, but not in a physical or painful way, if that makes sense? Just a sudden, absolute necessity to be vertical. Like yesterday, I was grading papers at the kitchen table—just AP History essays, which take forever, honestly, because the kids write these sprawling paragraphs and you really have to focus to follow their arguments—and my legs were totally fine, just resting on the rung of the stool. But I just had to stand up. I ended up taking the whole stack of rubrics and papers over to the island to grade standing up for the next twenty minutes. \n\nI didn't feel any physical relief in my legs when I stood, because they didn't hurt or feel restless to begin with, but I just felt... better, overall, being up. Like I was finally doing what I was supposed to be doing. So, no, definitely no uncomfortable feeling in my legs specifically. It's not a physical ache at all.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. I mean, sure, everyone's mind wanders a bit, especially during those long professional development days where they bring in some consultant who hasn't been in a classroom since 1995 to tell us how to engage teenagers. But it's not that I'm losing my train of thought and standing up brings it back. \n\nIt's actually almost the opposite? Like, I can be completely focused on what's happening. I could be totally engrossed in a student's presentation on the Industrial Revolution, or right in the middle of a really good conversation with my husband about our weekend plans, and I still just have to get up. My brain is right there in the moment, fully engaged, but my body just... rebels against the chair. \n\nI remember last week, we had a faculty meeting about the new grading software, which, let me tell you, is a nightmare to navigate, but I was actually really trying to pay attention because I needed to know how to input my midterm grades. I was taking notes, I was following along on my laptop, I was completely dialed in. And yet, I still found myself standing up and migrating to the back of the library. I didn't stand up to concentrate better on the software demonstration; I just stood up because I couldn't sit anymore. And honestly, sometimes getting up actually distracts me more because then I have to think of a reason why I'm standing, like pretending to look at the bulletin board, instead of just focusing on the meeting. So, no, I don't think it's a matter of my mind wandering and needing to reset my focus. It's just... the sitting itself that I can't seem to tolerate for very long.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Subtypes: Auditory Hallucinations, Visual Hallucinations, Tactile Hallucinations, Olfactory Hallucinations, Gustatory Hallucinations\n[S021] Disorganized_Thinking_Speech: Symptoms where speech or thought appears scattered and difficult to follow due to a lack of logical or consistent flow.  Subtypes: Derailment or Loose Associations, Tangentiality, Incoherence or Word Salad, Neologisms, Clang Associations\n[S022] Disorganized_Abnormal_Behavior: A state where behavior and motor control are severely disturbed, appearing as movements or reactions inappropriate to the situation.  Subtypes: Childlike Silliness, Stupor, Catalepsy, Waxy Flexibility, Mutism, Negativism, Posturing, Mannerism, Stereotypy, Agitation, Grimacing, Echolalia, Echopraxia\n[S023] Negative_Symptoms: A state where emotions, motivation, speech, or social interest that should normally be present are diminished or lost.  Subtypes: Diminished Emotional Expression, Avolition, Alogia, Anhedonia, Asociality\n[S024] Depressed_Mood: A state where a sad, empty, or hopeless mood persists.  Subtypes: Depressed Mood, Tearfulness, Sadness, Emptiness, Emotional Numbness\n[S025] Loss_of_Interest: A state where interest or pleasure in previously enjoyed activities is markedly diminished.  Subtypes: Loss of Interest, Social Withdrawal, Loss of Motivation, Anhedonia, Indifference to Achievement\n[S026] Weight_Appetite_Change: Significant increase or decrease in appetite or weight.  Subtypes: Significant Weight Loss, Weight Gain, Decrease in Appetite, Increase in Appetite\n[S027] Sleep_Disturbance: A state where there is a significant change in the quantity or quality of sleep.  Subtypes: Initial Insomnia, Middle Insomnia, Early Morning Awakening, Hypersomnia, Nightmare Disorder, Non-Restorative Sleep\n[S028] Psychomotor_Changes: Observable increase or decrease in psychomotor activity.  Subtypes: Psychomotor Agitation, Psychomotor Retardation (Motor), Psychomotor Retardation (Speech)\n[S029] Fatigue: Persistent fatigue that is not relieved by rest and a marked lack of energy.  Subtypes: Chronic Fatigue, Physical Fatigue, Mental Fatigue, Loss of Energy\n[S030] Worthlessness_Guilt: Feelings of worthlessness or excessive or inappropriate guilt (which may be delusional; not merely self-reproach or guilt about being sick).  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Ruminative Guilt, Excessive or Inappropriate Guilt, Delusional Guilt\n[S031] Diminished_Thinking: Diminished ability to think or concentrate, or indecisiveness.  Subtypes: Reduced Concentration, Indecisiveness, Subjective Memory Complaints, Clouded Thinking\n[S032] Death_Suicide: Recurrent thoughts of death, recurrent suicidal ideation, or a suicide attempt.  Subtypes: Recurrent Thoughts of Death, Suicidal Ideation, Suicidal Impulse, Specific Plan for Suicide, Suicide Attempt\n[S083] Hopelessness: A feeling of despair or having no hope for the future.  Subtypes: Future Hopelessness, Belief in Impossibility of Improvement, Helpless Resignation, Chronic Pessimism\n[S033] Elevated_Irritable_Mood: A distinct period of abnormally and persistently elevated, expansive, or irritable mood.  Subtypes: Elevated Mood, Irritable Mood, Mood Lability\n[S034] Increased_Activity: Abnormally and persistently increased goal-directed activity or energy.  Subtypes: Increased Energy, Hyperactivity, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S035] Inflated_Self_Esteem: Inflated self-esteem or grandiosity (may range from uncritical self-confidence to delusional proportions).  Subtypes: Inflated Self-Confidence, Overestimation of Abilities, Grandiose Delusions\n[S036] Decreased_Sleep: Decreased need for sleep (e.g., feels rested after only 3 hours of sleep).  Subtypes: Reduced Sleep Duration, Loss of Sleep Need, Indifference to Insomnia\n[S037] Pressured_Speech: More talkative than usual or pressure to keep talking.  Subtypes: Talkativeness, Rapid Speech, Pressured Speech\n[S038] Racing_Thoughts: Flight of ideas or subjective experience that thoughts are racing.  Subtypes: Racing Thoughts, Flooding of Thoughts, Inability to Control Thoughts\n[S039] Manic_Distractibility: Attention is too easily drawn to unimportant or irrelevant external stimuli.  Subtypes: Responsiveness to Extraneous Stimuli, Frequent Task Switching, Inability to Sustain Attention\n[S040] Increased_Goal_Activity: Increase in goal-directed activity (at work, school, socially, or sexually) or purposeless psychomotor agitation.  Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences.  Subtypes: Unrestrained Buying Sprees, Foolish Business Investments, Sexual Indiscretions, Impulsive Risky Behavior\n[S042] Low_Self_Esteem: A persistent tendency to evaluate oneself as incompetent or worthless; chronically low self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Self-Criticism, Reduced Self-Efficacy, Chronic Sense of Inferiority, Lack of Self-Assurance\n[S043] Restrictive_Intake: Persistent restriction of energy intake relative to requirements, leading to significantly low body weight.  Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health.  Subtypes: Continuous Weight Loss, Insistence on Low Weight\n[S045] Fear_of_Weight_Gain: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight.  Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Active Avoidance, Functional Impairment due to Avoidance, Enduring with Distress, Reliance on Safety Signals\n[S065] Obsessions: Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.  Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event.  Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\n{\n  \"key\": \"None\",\n  \"name\": \"None\",\n  \"description\": \"None\",\n  \"reason\": \"The doctor is asking about feeling unusually euphoric or excessively upbeat, which screens for mania or hypomania. Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nNo, no, definitely not. It's not a happy feeling at all. I wouldn't call it euphoric or upbeat in the slightest. It's actually quite the opposite, really. It's more of an annoyance, a frustration that I can't just be still like everyone else seems to be able to do. \n\nI mean, when I'm up and moving because of that internal pressure, I'm not doing it with a spring in my step or a song in my heart. I'm not whistling while I work, like some cheerful cartoon character. Take that time I was reorganizing the Tupperware drawer—I wasn't thrilled to be doing it. I was actually quite irritated because I *wanted* to be sitting on the patio reading about Eleanor Roosevelt. I *wanted* to be relaxing. But I was practically throwing the plastic lids around because I was just so aggravated by this need to be doing something. \n\nMy husband, bless him, sometimes he’ll see me pacing around the living room during a movie—we were trying to watch that new historical drama on PBS last night, beautifully costumed, really quite stunning, but I couldn't stay on the sofa for more than twenty minutes—and he'll ask if I'm excited about something. And I have to tell him, no, I'm not excited, I'm just... compelled. It's not a joyful energy. It's just a relentless one. So, no, there's no euphoria involved at all. Just a lot of sighing and wishing I could just sit still and enjoy a quiet moment without my body deciding it's time to run a marathon.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Err toward UNKNOWN (omit) rather than guessing.\n\nReply ONLY with valid JSON (no markdown, no extra text):\n{\n  \"confirmed\": [\"S001\", ...],\n  \"denied\": [\"S025\", ...],\n  \"reasoning\": {\n    \"S001\": \"one-sentence justification\",\n    ...\n  }\n}"
  }

Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Active Avoidance, Functional Impairment due to Avoidance, Enduring with Distress, Reliance on Safety Signals\n[S065] Obsessions: Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.  Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli. Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nAnxious? Well... I suppose it depends on how you define anxious, doesn't it? I mean, I'm not sitting there worrying about, oh, whether I left the stove on, or if my students are going to fail their state exams—though, frankly, with the way the curriculum is structured these days, that's a valid concern for any teacher. But it's not a fearful anxiety. It's not like my heart is pounding because I'm scared of something or because I'm having a panic attack. I've had colleagues who get panic attacks, and it sounds just awful, but this isn't that.\n\nBut, on the other hand, if you mean anxious as in... keyed up, or agitated? Then maybe yes. Because when I can't get up, when I'm forced to just sit there and endure that buzzing feeling I told you about, I do start to feel a bit frantic. Like, I remember being at my niece's graduation ceremony last spring. Oh, it was lovely, she looked beautiful, valedictorian and everything, but the ceremony was *three hours long*. Three hours! Sitting on those hard metal folding chairs in a stuffy gymnasium. And by the second hour, when they were only halfway through the M's, I was practically crawling out of my skin. I wasn't worried or scared, but I was so incredibly tense and agitated that I felt like I was going to scream if I couldn't stand up and stretch my legs. I kept crossing and uncrossing my ankles, shifting my weight, checking my watch every two minutes. \n\nMy sister kept giving me these side-eyes, you know the look, the \"stop fidgeting, you're embarrassing me\" look. And that made me feel a little stressed, I suppose, because I knew I was being disruptive, but I just couldn't help it. The longer I had to suppress the urge to move, the more unbearable it became. So, it's not a psychological anxiety, where my mind is racing with worries. It's more of a physical distress, a desperate need for release. I don't know if that's what you mean by anxious, but that's the closest I can get to describing it. It's just this overwhelming, physical imperative to move, and when I can't, it's sheer torture.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate.
